		</ul>
		
	</div>
</div>

					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content"><div class="mw-subjectpageheader">
</div><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1353705441">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+span.mw-empty-elt+.hatnote,.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the given name. For the family name, see <a href="/wiki/Charles_(surname)" title="Charles (surname)">Charles (surname)</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Charles_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Charles (disambiguation)">Charles (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1353403208">.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small">French:</span> <span class="IPA (disambiguation)">Carolus</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Related names</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Caroline_(name)" class="mw-redirect" title="Caroline (name)">Caroline</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charlotte_(given_name)" title="Charlotte (given name)">Charlotte</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charlene_(given_name)" title="Charlene (given name)">Charlene</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Charles</b> is a masculine <a href="/wiki/Given_name" title="Given name">given name</a> predominantly found in <a href="/wiki/English_language" title="English language">English</a> and <a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a> speaking countries.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>1<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup> It is from the French form <i>Charles</i> of the <a href="/wiki/Proto-Germanic" class="mw-redirect" title="Proto-Germanic">Proto-Germanic name</a> <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1227795492">.mw-parser-output .script-runic{font-family:"BabelStone Runic","FreeMono",Junicode,Code2000,Hnias,"Noto Sans Runic","San Francisco","New York"}</style><span class="script-runic" style="">ᚲᚨᚱᛁᛚᚨᛉ</span> (in <a href="/wiki/Runic_alphabet" class="mw-redirect" title="Runic alphabet">runic alphabet</a>) or <i>*karilaz</i> (in <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Latin alphabet</a>), whose meaning was "free man". The <a href="/wiki/Old_English" title="Old English">Old English</a> descendant of this word was <i><a href="/wiki/Churl" title="Churl">Ċearl</a></i> or <i>Ċeorl</i>, as the name of King <a href="/wiki/Cearl_of_Mercia" title="Cearl of Mercia">Cearl of Mercia</a>, that disappeared after the <a href="/wiki/Norman_conquest_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Norman conquest of England">Norman conquest of England</a>.
</p><p>The name was notably borne by <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a> (Charles the Great), and was at the time <a href="/wiki/Latinisation_of_names" title="Latinisation of names">Latinized</a> as <i>Karolus</i> (as in <i><a href="/wiki/Vita_Karoli_Magni" title="Vita Karoli Magni">Vita Karoli Magni</a></i>), later also as <i><a href="/wiki/Carolus_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Carolus (disambiguation)">Carolus</a></i>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc">
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Etymology">Etymology</h2></div>
<p>The name's etymology is a <a href="/wiki/Common_Germanic" class="mw-redirect" title="Common Germanic">Common Germanic</a> noun <i>*karilaz</i> meaning "free man", which survives in English as <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/churl" class="extiw" title="wikt:churl">churl</a> (&lt; Old English <i>ċeorl</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>2<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup> which developed its deprecating sense in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_English" title="Middle English">Middle English</a> period.
</p><p>Some Germanic languages, for example <a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a> and <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>, have  retained the word in two separate senses. In the particular case of Dutch, <i>Karel</i> refers to the <a href="/wiki/Given_name" title="Given name">given name</a>, whereas the noun <i>kerel</i> means "a bloke, fellow, man".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">[<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>]</sup>
</p><p>In the form <i>Charles</i>, the initial spelling <i>ch-</i> corresponds to the <a href="/wiki/Palatalization_(sound_change)" title="Palatalization (sound change)">palatalization</a> of the Latin group <i>ca-</i> to [tʃa] in Central Old French (<a href="/wiki/Francien_language" title="Francien language">Francien</a>) and the final <i>-s</i> to the former subjective case (cas sujet) of <a href="/wiki/Masculine_(grammar)" class="mw-redirect" title="Masculine (grammar)">masculine</a> names in <a href="/wiki/Old_French" title="Old French">Old French</a> like in <a href="/wiki/Giles_(given_name)" title="Giles (given name)">Giles</a> or <a href="/wiki/James_(given_name)" title="James (given name)">James</a> (&lt; Latin <i>-us</i>, see Spanish/ Portuguese <i>Carlos</i>).
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Julius_Pokorny" title="Julius Pokorny">Julius Pokorny</a>, the historical linguist and <a href="/wiki/Indo-European_studies" title="Indo-European studies">Indo-Europeanist</a>, the root meaning of Charles is "old man", from <a href="/wiki/Proto-Indo-European_language" title="Proto-Indo-European language">Indo-European</a> *<a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Appendix:Proto-Indo-European/%C7%B5erh%E2%82%82-" class="extiw" title="wikt:Appendix:Proto-Indo-European/ǵerh₂-">ĝer-</a>, where the ĝ is a <a href="/wiki/Palatal_consonant" title="Palatal consonant">palatal consonant</a>, meaning "to rub; to be old; grain." An old man has been worn away and is now grey with age.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">[<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag may need clarification or removal of jargon. (April 2018)">clarification needed</span></a></i>]</sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>3<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In some <a href="/wiki/Slavic_languages" title="Slavic languages">Slavic languages</a>, the name <i><a href="/wiki/Drago_(given_name)" title="Drago (given name)">Drago</a></i> (and variants: <i>Dragomir</i>, <i>Dragoslav</i>, etc., all based on the root <i>drag</i> 'dear') has been used as an equivalent for <i>Charles</i> (<i>Karel</i>, etc.). This is based on the <a href="/wiki/False_etymology" title="False etymology">false etymology</a> deriving <i>Carl</i> from <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a> <i>carus</i> 'dear'.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>4<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup> Examples are the Slovene politician <a href="/wiki/Karl_Deschmann" title="Karl Deschmann">Karl Deschmann</a> (1821–1889), also known as <i>Dragotin Dežman</i>, and the Slovene historian <a href="/wiki/Dragotin_Lon%C4%8Dar" title="Dragotin Lončar">Dragotin Lončar</a> (1876–1954), baptized <i>Carl</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>5<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Early_Middle_Ages">Early Middle Ages</h3></div>
<p>The name is atypical for <a href="/wiki/Germanic_name" title="Germanic name">Germanic names</a> as it is not composed of two elements, but simply a noun meaning "(free) man". This meaning of <i>ceorl</i> contrasts with <i><a href="/wiki/Earl" title="Earl">eorl</a></i> (Old Norse <i>jarl</i>) "nobleman" on one hand and with  <i>þeow</i> (Old Norse <i><a href="/wiki/Thrall" title="Thrall">þræll</a></i>) "bondsman, slave" on the other. As such it would not seem a likely candidate for the name of a <a href="/wiki/Germanic_king" class="mw-redirect" title="Germanic king">Germanic king</a>, but it is attested as such with <a href="/wiki/Cearl_of_Mercia" title="Cearl of Mercia">Cearl of Mercia</a> (<abbr title="floruit ('flourished'&nbsp;– known to have been active at a particular time or during a particular period)">fl.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;"> 620</span>),  the first Mercian king mentioned by <a href="/wiki/Bede" title="Bede">Bede</a> in his <i><a href="/wiki/Historia_ecclesiastica_gentis_Anglorum" class="mw-redirect" title="Historia ecclesiastica gentis Anglorum">Historia ecclesiastica gentis Anglorum</a></i>. It is a peculiarity of the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_genealogies" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Saxon genealogies">Anglo-Saxon royal names</a> that many of the rulers of the earliest period (6th to 7th centuries) have monothematic (simplex) names, while the standard <a href="/wiki/Dithematic_name" title="Dithematic name">dithematic names</a> become almost universal from the 8th century. Compare the name of King <a href="/wiki/Mul_of_Kent" title="Mul of Kent">Mul of Kent</a> (7th century) which simply translates to "mule".
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charles_Martel" title="Charles Martel">Charles Martel</a>, the son of <a href="/wiki/Pepin_of_Herstal" title="Pepin of Herstal">Pepin of Herstal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alpaida" title="Alpaida">Alpaida</a>, was either illegitimate or the product of a bigamous marriage, and therefore indeed a "free man", but not of noble rank. After his victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Soissons_(718)" title="Battle of Soissons (718)">Battle of Soissons (718)</a>, Charles Martel styled himself <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_the_Franks" title="Duke of the Franks">Duke of the Franks</a>. Charles' eldest son was named <a href="/wiki/Carloman_(mayor_of_the_palace)" title="Carloman (mayor of the palace)">Carloman</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;"> 710</span>&nbsp;– 754), a rare example of the element <i>carl-</i> occurring in a compound name. 
The <i><a href="/wiki/Chronicle_of_Fredegar" title="Chronicle of Fredegar">Chronicle of Fredegar</a></i> names an earlier <i>Carloman</i> as the father of <a href="/wiki/Pepin_of_Landen" title="Pepin of Landen">Pepin of Landen</a>, and thus the great-great-grandfather of the Charles Martel. This would place the name <i>Carloman</i> in the 6th century, and open the possibility that the Frankish name <i>Carl</i> may originate as a short form of <i>Carloman</i>. The only other compound name with the <i>Carl-</i> prefix is <i>Carlofred</i> (<i>Carlefred</i>), attested in the 7th century; as a suffix, it occurs in the rare names <i>Altcarl</i> and <i>Gundecarl</i> (9th and 11th centuries, respectively).<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>6<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a> (742–814) was Charles Martel's grandson. After Charlemagne's reign, the name became irrevocably connected with him and his <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_dynasty" title="Carolingian dynasty">Carolingian dynasty</a>.
After Charlemagne, the name Charles (<i>Karol</i>) became even the standard word for "king" in Slavic (Czech and Slovak <i>král</i>, Polish <i>król</i>; South Slavic <i>kral</i> крал, <i>krȃlj</i> краљ; Russian король), Baltic (Latvian <i>karalis</i>, Lithuanian <i>karalius</i>) and Hungarian (<i>király</i>).
</p><p>Charlemagne's son <a href="/wiki/Charles_the_Younger" title="Charles the Younger">Charles the Younger</a> died without issue, but the name resurfaces repeatedly within the 9th-century Carolingian family tree, so with <a href="/wiki/Charles_the_Bald" title="Charles the Bald">Charles the Bald</a> (823–877), <a href="/wiki/Charles_the_Fat" title="Charles the Fat">Charles the Fat</a> (839–888) <a href="/wiki/Charles_of_Provence" title="Charles of Provence">Charles of Provence</a> (845–863), <a href="/wiki/Charles_the_Child" title="Charles the Child">Charles the Child</a> (847/848–866) and <a href="/wiki/Charles_the_Simple" title="Charles the Simple">Charles the Simple</a> (879–929).
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Later_Middle_Ages_and_Early_Modern_history">Later Middle Ages and Early Modern history</h3></div>
<p>The name survives into the High Middle Ages (<a href="/wiki/Charles,_Duke_of_Lower_Lorraine" title="Charles, Duke of Lower Lorraine">Charles, Duke of Lower Lorraine</a>; <a href="/wiki/Charles,_Count_of_Valois" title="Charles, Count of Valois">Charles, Count of Valois</a>; <a href="/wiki/Charles_I,_Count_of_Flanders" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles I, Count of Flanders">Charles I, Count of Flanders</a> (Charles the Good, beatified in 1882); <a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_Naples" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles I of Naples">Charles I of Naples</a>; <a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_Hungary" title="Charles I of Hungary">Charles I of Hungary</a>). <a href="/wiki/Charles_VII_of_Sweden" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles VII of Sweden">Karl Sverkersson</a> was a king of Sweden in the 12th century, counted as "Charles VII" due to a genealogical fiction of the 17th century by <a href="/wiki/Charles_IX_of_Sweden" title="Charles IX of Sweden">Charles "IX"</a>, but actually the first king of Sweden with this name.
</p><p>Charles resurfaces as a royal name in Germany with <a href="/wiki/Charles_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles IV, Holy Roman Emperor</a> (1316–1378, counted as "the fourth" after Charlemagne, Charles the Bald and Charles the Fat) and in France with <a href="/wiki/Charles_IV_of_France" title="Charles IV of France">Charles IV of France</a> (1294–1328, "the fourth" after Charlemagne, Charles the Bald and Charles the Simple), and becomes comparatively widespread in the <a href="/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages" title="Late Middle Ages">Late Middle Ages</a> (<a href="/wiki/Charles_I,_Duke_of_Savoy" title="Charles I, Duke of Savoy">Charles I, Duke of Savoy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_III,_Duke_of_Savoy" title="Charles III, Duke of Savoy">Charles III, Duke of Savoy</a>).
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor</a> (1500–1558) gives rise to a tradition of Charlses in Habsburg Spain (<a href="/wiki/Charles_VI,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles VI, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles VI, Holy Roman Emperor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_II_of_Spain" title="Charles II of Spain">Charles II of Spain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_III_of_Spain" title="Charles III of Spain">Charles III of Spain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_IV_of_Spain" title="Charles IV of Spain">Charles IV of Spain</a>).
</p><p>The numbering scheme for the <a href="/wiki/Kings_of_Sweden" class="mw-redirect" title="Kings of Sweden">kings of Sweden</a> was continued in modern times with <a href="/wiki/Charles_X_Gustav_of_Sweden" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles X Gustav of Sweden">Charles X Gustav</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_XI_of_Sweden" title="Charles XI of Sweden">Charles XI</a>,  <a href="/wiki/Charles_XII_of_Sweden" title="Charles XII of Sweden">Charles XII</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_XIII_of_Sweden" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles XIII of Sweden">Charles XIII</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_XIV_John" title="Charles XIV John">Charles XIV John</a> and <a href="/wiki/Charles_XV_of_Sweden" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles XV of Sweden">Charles XV</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_England" title="Charles I of England">Charles I of England</a> (1600–1649) is followed by <a href="/wiki/Charles_II_of_England" title="Charles II of England">Charles II of England</a> (1630–1685). The <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Carolina" title="Province of Carolina">Province of Carolina</a> is named during the rule of Charles II, after Charles I.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charles_III_Philip,_Elector_Palatine" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles III Philip, Elector Palatine">Charles III Philip, Elector Palatine</a> (1661–1742).
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Modern_history">Modern history</h3></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Carlism" title="Carlism">Carlism</a> is a political movement in <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a> seeking the establishment of a separate line of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Bourbon" title="House of Bourbon">Bourbon</a> family on the <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_Spain" title="Monarchy of Spain">Spanish throne</a>. This line descended from <a href="/wiki/Infante_Carlos,_Count_of_Molina" class="mw-redirect" title="Infante Carlos, Count of Molina">Infante Carlos, Count of Molina</a> (1788–1855), and was founded due to dispute over the succession laws and widespread dissatisfaction with the Alfonsine line of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Bourbon" title="House of Bourbon">House of Bourbon</a>. The movement was at its strongest in the 1830s, causing the <a href="/wiki/Carlist_Wars" title="Carlist Wars">Carlist Wars</a>, and had a revival following Spain's defeat in the <a href="/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War" title="Spanish–American War">Spanish–American War</a> in 1898, and lasted until the end of the <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Franco" title="Francisco Franco">Franco</a> regime in 1975 as a social and political force
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charles_Floyd_(explorer)" title="Charles Floyd (explorer)">Charles Floyd</a> (1782–1804) was the only casualty  in the <a href="/wiki/Lewis_and_Clark_Expedition" title="Lewis and Clark Expedition">Lewis and Clark Expedition</a>. <a href="/wiki/Charles_DeRudio" title="Charles DeRudio">Charles DeRudio</a> (1832–1910) was an Italian aristocrat, would-be assassin of <a href="/wiki/Napoleon_III" title="Napoleon III">Napoleon III</a>, and later a career U.S. Army officer who fought in the <a href="/wiki/7th_U.S._Cavalry" class="mw-redirect" title="7th U.S. Cavalry">7th U.S. Cavalry</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Little_Bighorn" title="Battle of the Little Bighorn">Battle of the Little Bighorn</a>. <a href="/wiki/Charles_Albert_Varnum" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles Albert Varnum">Charles Albert Varnum</a> (1849–1936) was the commander of the scouts in the Little Bighorn Campaign and received the <a href="/wiki/Medal_of_Honor" title="Medal of Honor">Medal of Honor</a> for his actions in a conflict following the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Wounded_Knee" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Wounded Knee">Battle of Wounded Knee</a>.
<a href="/wiki/Charley_Reynolds" title="Charley Reynolds">"Lonesome" Charley Reynolds</a> (1842–1876) was a scout in the U.S. 7th Cavalry Regiment who was killed at the Battle of the Little Bighorn.
</p><p><i>Carl</i> has been a very popular male given name in the United States during the late 19th to early 20th centuries, consistently ranking in the top 30 male given names in the US from 1887 to 1938, and remaining among the top 100 until the 1980s, but since declining below rank 500. <i>Charles</i> has always been among the top 100 names in the U.S. since records started in 1880.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>7<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup> In addition, it is among the top 100 names given in England and Wales; the current <a href="/wiki/King_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="King of the United Kingdom">King of the United Kingdom</a> and the other Commonwealth realms, <a href="/wiki/Charles_III" title="Charles III">Charles III</a>, is a notable bearer of the name. In 2022, it was the 26th most popular name given to boys in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>8<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Derived_feminine_names">Derived feminine names</h2></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Caroline_(given_name)" title="Caroline (given name)">Caroline</a> and <b>Charlotte</b> are feminine given names derived from <i>Carl</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>11<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Charlotte_(given_name)" title="Charlotte (given name)">Charlotte</a> is late medieval, e.g. <a href="/wiki/Charlotte_of_Savoy" title="Charlotte of Savoy">Charlotte of Savoy</a> (1441–1483), <a href="/wiki/Charlotte_of_Cyprus" class="mw-redirect" title="Charlotte of Cyprus">Charlotte of Cyprus</a> (1444–1487). It was introduced to Britain in the 17th century, and gave rise to hypocorisms such as <i>Lottie, Tottie, Totty</i>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Caroline_(given_name)" title="Caroline (given name)">Caroline</a> is early modern,  e.g. <a href="/wiki/Caroline_of_Ansbach" title="Caroline of Ansbach">Caroline of Ansbach</a> (1683–1737). It has  given rise to numerous variations, such as <i> Carlyn, Carolina, Carolyn, Karolyn, Carolin, Karolina, Karoline, Karolina, Carolien</i>, as well as hypocorisms, such as <i>Callie, Carol, Carrie</i>, etc.
</p><p>Another derived feminine name is <b>Carla</b> (<a href="/wiki/Bulgarian_language" title="Bulgarian language">Bulgarian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catalan_language" title="Catalan language">Catalan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dutch_language" title="Dutch language">Dutch</a>, <a href="/wiki/English_language" title="English language">English</a>, <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a>, <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Spanish</a>), a name which dates from early Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12"><span class="cite-bracket">[</span>12<span class="cite-bracket">]</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Regional forms:
</p>
<ul><li><ul><li>Carolina (Italian, Indonesian, Portuguese, Spanish, Swedish, Bulgarian)</li>
<li>Caroline (English, French, Indonesian, Swedish, Danish, Dutch)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Carolyn" title="Carolyn">Carolyn</a> (English)</li>
<li>Carlijn (Dutch)</li>
<li>Karoliina (Finnish)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Karolina_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Karolina (disambiguation)">Karolina</a> (Bulgarian, Indonesian, Polish, Swedish)</li>
<li>Karolína (Czech)</li>
<li>Karoline (Danish, Indonesian, Norwegian, Swedish)</li>
<li>Karolina (Каролина) (Russian)</li>
<li>Keraleyn (קעראַליין) (Yiddish)</li>
<li>Carly (American)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Carol_(given_name)" title="Carol (given name)">Carol</a> (English)</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Carola" title="Carola">Carola</a> (German, Swedish)
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Carole_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect" title="Carole (disambiguation)">Carole</a> (English, French, Portuguese)</li>
<li>Karol (קאַראָל) (<a href="/wiki/Yiddish_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Yiddish language">Yiddish</a>)</li>
<li>Kyārōla (क्यारोल) (<a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali</a>)</li>
<li>Kerol (Керол) (Serbian), (Russian)</li></ul></li>
<li>Charlotte (English, French, German, Swedish, Danish, Dutch)
<ul><li>Carlota (Spanish, Portuguese, Catalan)</li>

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="In_religion">In religion</h3></div>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Barff_(missionary)" title="Charles Barff (missionary)">Charles Barff</a> (1791–1866), English missionary</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_S._Levi" title="Charles S. Levi">Charles S. Levi</a> (1868–1939), English-American rabbi</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Saints">Saints</h4></div>
<p><span class="anchor" id="Saint_Charles"></span>
There are a number of historical figures known as "Saint Charles", although few are recognized across confessions. 
In the context of English and British history, "Saint Charles" is typically <a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_England" title="Charles I of England">Charles I of England</a>, recognized as a saint in the Anglican confession only. 
In Roman Catholicism, the best known Saint Charles is <a href="/wiki/Charles_Borromeo" title="Charles Borromeo">Charles Borromeo</a> (1538–1584), an Italian cardinal, canonized by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_V" title="Pope Paul V">Pope Paul V</a> in 1606.
<a href="/wiki/Charles,_Duke_of_Brittany" title="Charles, Duke of Brittany">Charles, Duke of Brittany</a> (1319–1364) had been canonized after his death, but <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XI" title="Pope Gregory XI">Pope Gregory XI</a> annulled this. <a href="/wiki/Charles_I,_Count_of_Flanders" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles I, Count of Flanders">Charles the Good</a> (died 1127) is sometimes referred to as a saint, but while he was beatified in 1904, he has not been canonized.
</p><p>Other Saints of the Roman Catholic Church, canonized after 1900:
</p>
<ul><li>1904: Saint <a href="/wiki/Charles_Garnier_(missionary)" title="Charles Garnier (missionary)">Charles Garnier</a> (1606–1649), French Jesuit missionary and martyr</li>
<li>1959: Saint <a href="/wiki/Charles_of_Sezze" title="Charles of Sezze">Charles of Sezze</a> (1616–1670), Franciscan lay brother</li>
<li>1964: Saint <a href="/wiki/Charles_Lwanga" title="Charles Lwanga">Charles Lwanga</a> (1860 or 1865–1886), Ugandan Catholic martyr</li>
<li>1995: Saint <a href="/wiki/Eug%C3%A8ne_de_Mazenod" title="Eugène de Mazenod">Charles-Joseph-Eugène de Mazenod</a> (1782–1861), French Catholic clergyman</li>
<li>2007: Saint <a href="/wiki/Charles_of_Mount_Argus" title="Charles of Mount Argus">Charles of Mount Argus</a> (1821–1893), Passionist Dutch priest who worked in Ireland</li></ul>
<p>Beatified:
</p>
<ul><li>1867: Blessed <a href="/wiki/Charles_Spinola" title="Charles Spinola">Charles Spinola</a> (1564–1622), Genoese nobleman</li>
<li>2004: Blessed <a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_Austria" title="Charles I of Austria">Charles I of Austria</a> (1887–1922), last emperor of <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria</a>, king of <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary_(Austria-Hungary)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Hungary (Austria-Hungary)">Hungary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bohemia_(1867%E2%80%931918)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Bohemia (1867–1918)">Bohemia</a>, etc</li></ul>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 id="Church_leaders">Church leaders</h4></div>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Wesley" title="Charles Wesley">Charles Wesley</a> (1707–1788), co-founder of the Methodist movement and writer of thousands of hymns</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Grandison_Finney" title="Charles Grandison Finney">Charles Grandison Finney</a> (1792–1875), a leader of the Second Great Awakening in America</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Godwyn" title="Charles Godwyn">Charles Godwyn</a> (1701-1770), British cleric and antiquarian</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_W._Penrose" title="Charles W. Penrose">Charles W. Penrose</a> (1832–1925), leader in The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Haddon_Spurgeon" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles Haddon Spurgeon">Charles Haddon Spurgeon</a> (1834–1892), Reformed Baptist preacher</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Harrison_Mason" title="Charles Harrison Mason">Charles Harrison Mason</a> (1866–1961), <a href="/wiki/Pentecostal" class="mw-redirect" title="Pentecostal">Pentecostal</a> <a href="/wiki/Preacher" title="Preacher">preacher</a> and founder of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_God_in_Christ" title="Church of God in Christ">Church of God in Christ</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Coughlin" title="Charles Coughlin">Charles Coughlin</a> (1891–1979), American Catholic priest</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_E._Sheedy" title="Charles E. Sheedy">Charles E. Sheedy</a> (1912–1990), member of the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_of_Holy_Cross" title="Congregation of Holy Cross">Congregation of Holy Cross</a></li></ul>

<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Zadok" title="Charles Zadok">Charles Zadok</a> (1897–1984), American businessman, art collector and patron</li></ul>
<dl><dt>Military personnel</dt></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Charles_L._Armstrong" title="Charles L. Armstrong">Charles L. Armstrong</a> (1948-2011), United States Marine Corps lieutenant colonel</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_M._Betts" title="Charles M. Betts">Charles M. Betts</a> (1838-1905), American Medal of Honor recipient</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Upham" title="Charles Upham">Charles Upham</a>, most-decorated Commonwealth serviceman of World War Two</li></ul>
<dl><dt>Criminals</dt></dl>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Bronson_(prisoner)" title="Charles Bronson (prisoner)">Charles A. Salvador</a> (born Michael Peterson but better known as "Charles Bronson"), notorious English prisoner</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Cullen" title="Charles Cullen">Charles Cullen</a>, American serial killer and former nurse</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Gibbs" title="Charles Gibbs">Charles Gibbs</a>, 19th-century pirate</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_J._Guiteau" title="Charles J. Guiteau">Charles J. Guiteau</a>, American assassin of 20th President <a href="/wiki/James_A._Garfield" title="James A. Garfield">James A. Garfield</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Harrelson" title="Charles Harrelson">Charles Harrelson</a> (1938–2007), American hitman</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Hudspeth_(convict)" title="Charles Hudspeth (convict)">Charles Hudspeth</a>, American man convicted of murder</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Murder_of_the_Lawson_family" class="mw-redirect" title="Murder of the Lawson family">Charles "Charlie" Lawson</a>, American mass murderer and family annihilator</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Luciano" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles Luciano">Charles "Lucky" Luciano</a>, Italian-American mobster</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Manson" title="Charles Manson">Charles Manson</a> (1934–2017), American cult leader, convicted murder conspirator</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Nicoletti" title="Charles Nicoletti">Charles Nicoletti</a>, American mobster</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Carl_Panzram" title="Carl Panzram">Charles "Carl" Panzram</a>, American serial killer</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Ponzi" title="Charles Ponzi">Charles Ponzi</a>, Italian-American con-man, gave name to Ponzi scheme</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Sobhraj" title="Charles Sobhraj">Charles Sobhraj</a>, Indian serial killer</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Starkweather" title="Charles Starkweather">Charles Starkweather</a>, American teenage spree killer</li>
